\displaystyle={\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{k}\circ({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}_{2g-k}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}_{\leq k}^{d}+\sum_{i>2g-k}{\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{2g-i}\circ({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}_{i}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}_{\leq k}^{d}

where the second identity holds both by (
\frac{1}{2}
FV1) and for dimension reasons. Now the condition
i>2g-k

translates to
2g-i<k
, which implies that the entire second term in (
46
) factors through

{{}^{\mathfrak{p}}}\tau_{\leq k+\dim B}(\pi_{d*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{\overline{J}_{C}^{d}})\to{{}^{\mathfrak{p}}}\tau_{\leq k-1+\dim B}(\pi_{d*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{\overline{J}_{C}^{d}}).

Therefore we have

\mathrm{id}=\overline{{\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{k}}\circ\overline{({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}_{2g-k}}:{\mathcal{H}}^{d}_{(k)}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{d}_{(k)}.

The other direction is parallel. This proves (i).

For (ii) we consider via (
38
) the identity

*^{d}=({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}\circ\cup\circ({\mathfrak{F}}^{d}\otimes{\mathfrak{F}}^{d}).

For dimension reasons we then have

(47)

*_{\mathrm{red}}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}_{2g-i-j}\circ\cup\circ({\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{i}\otimes{\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{j}).

Again, in view of the realization Theorem
2.2
, to prove (ii) it suffices to show

{\mathfrak{q}}^{\vee}_{\geq k+l+1-2g}\circ*_{\mathrm{red}}\circ({\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}_{\leq k}\otimes{\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}_{\leq l})=0

where
{\mathfrak{q}}^{\vee}_{\geq k+l+1-2g}
is defined in (
31
). We expand
*_{\mathrm{red}}
using (
47
), and find

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}^{\vee}_{\geq k+l+1-2g}\circ*_{\mathrm{red}}\circ({\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}_{\leq k}\otimes{\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}_{\leq l})

\displaystyle{}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}{\mathfrak{q}}^{\vee}_{\geq k+l+1-2g}\circ({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}_{2g-i-j}\circ\cup\circ\left(({\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{i}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}_{\leq k})\otimes({\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{j}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}_{\leq l})\right)

\displaystyle{}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 2g-k}\sum_{j\geq 2g-l}{\mathfrak{q}}^{\vee}_{\geq k+l+1-2g}\circ({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}_{2g-i-j}\circ\cup\circ\left(({\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{i}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}_{\leq k})\otimes({\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{j}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}_{\leq l})\right)

where the second identity uses (
\frac{1}{2}
FV2). This time the conditions
i\geq 2g-k
and
j\geq 2g-l
translate to

2g-i-j\leq k+l-2g.

But then we have

{\mathfrak{q}}^{\vee}_{\geq k+l+1-2g}\circ({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}_{2g-i-j}=0

for such
i,j,k,l
by (
\frac{1}{2}
FV2). This proves (ii).

For (iii) we consider the following diagram

(48)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}^{\vee}_{(2g-k)}[2g-2k]\otimes{\mathcal{H}}^{\vee}_{(2g-l)}[2g-2l]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}^{\vee}_{(2g-k-l)}[2g-2k-2l]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}^{d}_{(k)}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}^{d}_{(l)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}^{d}_{(k+l)},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{*}_{\mathrm{red}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{k}}\otimes\overline{{\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{k+l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\cup}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})_{2g-k}^{-1}}\otimes\overline{({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})_{2g-l}^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})_{2g-k-l}^{-1}}}

where the top and bottom rows are given by (
43
) and (
33
), respectively, and the vertical arrows are isomorphisms by (
42
). We claim that the diagram commutes. Indeed,
decomposing

*^{d}=\sum_{c\geq g}*^{d}_{c},\quad*^{d}_{c}:\pi^{\vee}_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{J^{\vee}_{C}}\otimes\pi^{\vee}_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{J^{\vee}_{C}}\to\pi^{\vee}_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{J^{\vee}_{C}}[{2c-4g}]

according to the codimension, we have
*^{d}_{g}=*_{\mathrm{red}}
. There are identities

\displaystyle\cup\circ({\mathfrak{p}}^{d}_{\leq k}\otimes{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}_{\leq l})

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{F}}^{d}\circ*^{d}\circ\left((({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}_{\leq k})\otimes(({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}_{\leq l})\right)

(49)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c\geq g}\sum_{i\geq 2g-k}\sum_{j\geq 2g-l}{\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{5g-i-j-c}\circ*^{d}_{c}\circ\left((({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}_{i}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}_{\leq k})\otimes(({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})^{-1}_{j}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}_{\leq l})\right)

where the second identity uses both (
\frac{1}{2}
FV1) and dimension constraints. We observe that (
49
) contains the term

{\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{k+l}\circ*_{\mathrm{red}}\circ\left((({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})_{2g-k}^{-1}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}_{\leq k})\otimes(({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})_{2g-l}^{-1}\circ{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}_{\leq l})\right)

and all the other terms satisfy

5g-i-j-c<k+l.

By (i), this means that all the other terms in (
49
) factor through

{{}^{\mathfrak{p}}}\tau_{\leq k+\dim B}(\pi_{d*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{\overline{J}_{C}^{d}})\otimes{{}^{\mathfrak{p}}}\tau_{\leq l+\dim B}(\pi_{d*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{\overline{J}_{C}^{d}})\to{{}^{\mathfrak{p}}}\tau_{\leq k+l-1+\dim B}(\pi_{d*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{\overline{J}_{C}^{d}})

and hence descend to zero. This shows the commutativity of the diagram (
48
).

Finally, for any integer
d
, we conclude from (i) that there are mutually inverse graded isomorphisms

(50)

\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\vee}:=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{2g}{\mathcal{H}}^{\vee}_{(2g-k)}[2g-2k]\xrightleftharpoons[\oplus\overline{({\mathfrak{F}}^{d})_{2g-k}^{-1}}]{\oplus\overline{{\mathfrak{F}}^{d}_{k}}}\bigoplus_{k=0}^{2g}{\mathcal{H}}^{d}_{(k)}={\mathcal{H}}^{d}.

Moreover, by the commutative diagram (
48
), the above isomorphisms are compatible with the reduced convolution product
\overline{*}_{\mathrm{red}}
on the left-hand side, and the cup product
\overline{\cup}
on the right-hand side. This proves (iii) since by Proposition
2.5
, the product
\overline{*}_{\mathrm{red}}
on the left-hand side is independent of
d
. The proof of Theorem
2.6
is now complete.
∎

We deduce immediately the
d
-independence of the associated graded
{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
in (
41
) by taking global cohomology.

Corollary 2.7
.

For integers
d,d^{\prime}
, there is an isomorphism

{\mathbb{H}}^{d}\simeq{\mathbb{H}}^{d^{\prime}}

of bigraded
H^{*}(B,{\mathbb{Q}})
-algebras.

Remark 2.8
.

It is natural to ask if Corollary
2.7
also holds at the Chow level. The question is whether there is a
canonically defined
Chow-theoretic perverse filtration
P_{\bullet}\mathrm{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{C}^{\vee})
, which in particular satisfies

P_{k}\mathrm{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{C}^{\vee})=\mathrm{Im}\left({\mathfrak{p}}^{\vee}_{\leq k}:\mathrm{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{C}^{\vee})\to\mathrm{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{C}^{\vee})\right)

regardless of the degree
d
; see also Remark
2.3
. The answer is expected to be yes assuming certain Bloch–Beilinson type motivic conjectures in the relative setting.

3.
Fine compactified Jacobians

As we see in Section
2
, the main geometric ingredients in treating the associated graded with respect to the perverse filtration are

(i)

Ngô’s full support theorem;

(ii)

a theory of Fourier transforms.

In the first part of this section we describe a general framework handling the cohomology ring of a dualizable abelian fibration, extending
[
35
]
. Then in the second part we apply this to fine compactified Jacobians of reduced locally planar curves both in the global and the local settings, and prove Theorems
0.7
and
0.9
respectively.

Remark 3.1
.

Strictly speaking, the cases discussed in Sections
1
and
2
fall outside the framework of dualizable abelian fibrations. Instead, they can be viewed as “twisted” variants. Because these sections focus on degree independence, where normalizations and gerbes play a crucial role, we address them explicitly and do not incorporate them into the general framework. This clarifies the main ideas while also helping to avoid overly heavy notation in the framework.

3.1.
Dualizable abelian fibrations

We recall the notion of dualizable abelian fibration following
[
35
]
.

We say that
\pi:M\to B
is an
abelian fibration
if both
M
and
B
are nonsingular varieties,
\pi
is flat and proper, and the restriction of
M
to a certain nonempty open subset

\pi_{U}:M_{U}\to U\subset B

is an abelian scheme.

We say that
\pi^{\vee}:M^{\vee}\to B
is dual to the abelian fibration
\pi:M\to B
, if
\pi^{\vee}:M^{\vee}\to B
is an abelian fibration and there exists an open subset
U\subset B
over which
\pi
and
\pi^{\vee}
form dual abelian schemes. We say that

{{\mathcal{P}}}\in D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(M^{\vee}\times_{B}M)

is a
normalized Poincaré complex
, if the restriction of
{\mathcal{P}}
to
M_{U}^{\vee}\times_{U}M_{U}
recovers the normalized Poincaré line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
. Here
M_{U},M_{U}^{\vee}
are dual abelian schemes over some open
U\subset B
.

Definition 3.2

(Dualizable abelian fibration
[
35
]
)
.

A dualizable abelian fibration consists of the following data

(M,B,M^{\vee},{\mathcal{P}},{\mathcal{K}}).

Here
\pi:M\to B
is an abelian fibration of relative dimension
g
with a dual abelian fibration
\pi^{\vee}:M^{\vee}\to B
satisfying the following.

(a1)

(Poincaré) There is a normalized Poincaré complex
{{\mathcal{P}}}\in D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(M^{\vee}\times_{B}M)
which admits an inverse
{{\mathcal{P}}}^{-1}\in D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(M\times_{B}M^{\vee})
,
i.e.
,

{{\mathcal{P}}}^{-1}\circ{{\mathcal{P}}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{M^{\vee}/B}},\quad{{\mathcal{P}}}\circ{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{M/B}}.

(a2)

(Convolution) There is an object
{\mathcal{K}}\in D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee})
supported in codimension
\geq g
, which satisfies

(51)

{\mathcal{P}}\circ{\mathcal{K}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta^{\mathrm{sm}}_{M/B}}\circ({{\mathcal{P}}}\boxtimes{{\mathcal{P}}}).

Here
\Delta^{\mathrm{sm}}_{M/B}\subset M\times_{B}M\times_{B}M
is the small relative diagonal, and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta^{\mathrm{sm}}_{M/B}}\circ({{\mathcal{P}}}\boxtimes{{\mathcal{P}}})
stands for composing
{\mathcal{P}}
with
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta^{\mathrm{sm}}_{M/B}}
via the first (resp. second) factor of
M\times_{B}M\times_{B}M
. We refer to
{\mathcal{K}}
as the
convolution kernel
.

(b)

(Support) Both morphisms
\pi,\pi^{\vee}
have full support,
i.e.
, every simple perverse sheaf that appears in the pushforward complex
\pi_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{M}
or
\pi^{\vee}_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{M^{\vee}}
has support
B
.

We denote by
\mathrm{FM}_{\mathcal{P}}:D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(M^{\vee})\to D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(M)
the Fourier–Mukai transform associated with
{\mathcal{P}}
. Then the object
{\mathcal{K}}
in (a2) induces a convolution product

\ast:D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(M^{\vee})\times D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(M^{\vee})\to D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(M^{\vee}),\quad F\ast G=p_{3*}(p_{1}^{*}F\otimes p_{2}^{*}G\otimes{\mathcal{K}})

where the
p_{i}:M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\to M^{\vee}
are the three projections. We have by definition

\mathrm{FM}_{{\mathcal{P}}}(F\ast G)\simeq\mathrm{FM}_{{\mathcal{P}}}(F)\otimes\mathrm{FM}_{{\mathcal{P}}}(G),\quad F,G\in D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(M^{\vee}).

Remark 3.3
.

We refer to
[
35
, Section 1]
for more details and explanations on dualizable abelian fibrations. Note that there is a minor difference between the definition above and the one in
[
35
]
. The condition (b) in
[
35
, Definition 1.2]
only requires that
\pi
has full support; here for our purpose we need both maps
\pi,\pi^{\vee}
to have full support in order to relate the projection operators on each side with the perverse filtration.

Following
[
35
, Section 2.4]
(see also Section
2.2
), the Poincaré complex
{\mathcal{P}}
and its inverse
{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}
induce the Chow-theoretic Fourier transform

{\mathfrak{F}}=\sum_{i}{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}\in\mathrm{CH}_{*}(M^{\vee}\times_{B}M),\quad{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}\in\mathrm{CH}_{2g-i+\dim B}(M^{\vee}\times_{B}M)

and its inverse

{\mathfrak{F}}^{-1}=\sum_{i}{\mathfrak{F}}^{-1}_{i}\in\mathrm{CH}_{*}(M\times_{B}M^{\vee}),\quad{\mathfrak{F}}^{-1}_{i}\in\mathrm{CH}_{2g-i+\dim B}(M\times_{B}M^{\vee});

they also act sheaf-theoretically as in Section
2.2
.

3.2.
Cup-product, Fourier vanishing, and convolution

We define the shifted perverse sheaves
{\mathcal{H}}_{(k)}
associated with the decomposition of the pushforward complex
\pi_{*}{{\mathbb{Q}}_{M}}
as in (
26
); a key observation of
[
35
]
is that the compatibility of the perverse truncation functors and the cup-product (a.k.a. multiplicitivity) for dualizable abelian fibrations is governed by certain Fourier vanishing results.

Definition 3.4

(
\frac{1}{2}
Fourier vanishing)
.

The conditions (
\frac{1}{2}
FV1) and (
\frac{1}{2}
FV2) refer to the following statements: for all
i+j<2g
, we have

(
\frac{1}{2}
FV1)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}^{-1}_{j}\circ{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}=0\in\mathrm{CH}_{3g-i-j+\dim B}(M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}),

(
\frac{1}{2}
FV2)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}\circ{\mathfrak{F}}^{-1}_{i}=0\in\mathrm{CH}_{3g-i-j+\dim B}(M\times_{B}M).

By
[
35
, Theorem 2.6(iii)]
, if the dualizable abelian fibration satisfies (
\frac{1}{2}
FV1), then the multiplicitivity holds for the perverse truncation functors. In particular, we obtain the following.

Theorem 3.5
.

Assume that the dualizable abelian fibration

(M,B,M^{\vee},{\mathcal{P}},{\mathcal{K}})

satisfies
(
\frac{1}{2}
FV1)
. Then the cup-product on
\pi_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{M}
induces a cup-product on the associated graded

\overline{\cup}:{\mathcal{H}}_{M}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{M}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{M},\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{M}:=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{2g}{\mathcal{H}}_{(k)}\in D^{b}_{c}(B).

In particular, we may view
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}\in D^{b}_{c}(B)
as a graded ring object endowed with
\overline{\cup}
.

Theorem
3.5
only needs (
\frac{1}{2}
FV1) on
M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}
. The argument in Section
2
shows that, if we also have the other (
\frac{1}{2}
FV2), then we may describe the graded ring object
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
associated with
M
using the convolution kernel
{\mathcal{K}}
on
M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}
.

More precisely, we consider the Chow-theoretic convolution class

{\mathfrak{C}}:={\mathfrak{F}}^{-1}\circ[\Delta^{\mathrm{sm}}_{M/B}]\circ({\mathfrak{F}}\times{\mathfrak{F}})\in\mathrm{CH}_{*}(M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}).

Using (
51
) and singular Riemann–Roch (see
[
35
, Lemma 2.8]
), we also have

{\mathfrak{C}}={\mathrm{td}}(-p^{*}_{12}T_{M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}})\cap\tau({\mathcal{K}})

where
p_{12}:M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\to M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}
is the projection to the first two factors,
T_{M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}}
is the virtual tangent bundle of the l.c.i. scheme
M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}
, and
\tau(-)
is the Baum–Fulton–MacPherson tau-class. In particular, the dimension bound in Definition
3.2
(a2) implies

{\mathfrak{C}}\in\mathrm{CH}_{\leq 2g+\dim B}(M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}).

We then consider the codimension
g
component of
{\mathfrak{C}}
:

(52)

{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathrm{red}}\in\mathrm{CH}_{2g+\dim B}(M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee})

which we call the
reduced
convolution class.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
(M,B,M^{\vee},{\mathcal{P}},{\mathcal{K}})
be a dualizable abelian fibration satisfying both
(
\frac{1}{2}
FV1)
and
(
\frac{1}{2}
FV2)
. Then the graded ring object
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}\in D^{b}_{c}(B)
is completely determined by the reduced convolution class
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathrm{red}}
in (
52
). In particular, given two dualizable abelian fibrations

(M,B,M^{\vee},{\mathcal{P}},{\mathcal{K}}),\quad(M^{\prime},B,M^{\vee},{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})

satisfying
(
\frac{1}{2}
FV1)
and
(
\frac{1}{2}
FV2)
, we have two convolution classes

{\mathfrak{C}},{\mathfrak{C}}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{CH}_{\leq 2g+\dim B}(M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee})

induced by
{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
respectively. If we have a match for the reduced parts

{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathrm{red}}={\mathfrak{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{red}}\in\mathrm{CH}_{2g+\dim B}(M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}),

then there is an isomorphism

{\mathcal{H}}_{M}\simeq{\mathcal{H}}_{M^{\prime}}\in D^{b}_{c}(B)

of graded ring objects.

Proof.

The proof is essentially identical to the one given for compactified Jacobians associated with integral curves in Section
2
. For a dualizable abelian fibration

(M,B,M^{\vee},{\mathcal{P}},{\mathcal{K}}),

we show that the graded ring object
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
is isomorphic via the Fourier transform to
\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{M^{\vee}}
endowed with the convolution product induced by
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathrm{red}}
, as in (
50
). The proof of Theorem
2.6
works
verbatim
, since the only geometric inputs required are

(i)

the dimension bound for the support of the convolution kernel
{\mathcal{K}}
,

(ii)

the
\frac{1}{2}
Fourier vanishing, and

(iii)

the full support property of
\pi,\pi^{\vee}
,

all of which remain valid in the general setting. The rest of the argument is entirely formal.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
3.6
, statements parallel to Theorem
2.6
hold. Our result also shows that the induced cup product on the associated graded of the cohomology with respect to the perverse filtration

{\mathbb{H}}_{M}:=\bigoplus_{k,m}\mathrm{Gr}_{k}^{P}H^{m}(M,{\mathbb{Q}})

only relies on the reduced convolution class
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\mathrm{red}}
on
M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}\times_{B}M^{\vee}
. As in Remark
2.8
, the parallel statements at the Chow level is conditional on certain Bloch–Beilinson type motivic conjectures.

In the next sections, we apply Theorem
3.6
to fine compactified Jacobians over the moduli space
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
of stable marked curves, and for versal deformations of a reduced locally planar curve, which completes the proofs of Theorems
0.7
and
0.9
.

3.3.
Relative fine compactified Jacobians

Let
C\to B
be a flat projective family of connected reduced locally planar curves of arithmetic genus
g
over a nonsingular base variety
B
, with a section
s:B\to C
through the smooth locus of
C\to B
. Let
g:J^{\underline{0}}_{C}\to B
be the relative Picard space parameterizing pairs

(C_{b},L_{b}),\quad L_{b}\in J^{\underline{0}}_{C_{b}},

where
C_{b}\subset C
is the fiber over a closed point
b\in B
and
L_{b}
is a multidegree
0
line bundle on
C_{b}
. The commutative group scheme
J^{\underline{0}}_{C_{b}}
is irreducible, admitting a Chevalley decomposition

0\to R_{b}\to J^{\underline{0}}_{C_{b}}\to A_{b}\to 0

with
R_{b}
an commutative affine group and
A_{b}
an abelian variety. This defines a constructible function

\delta:B\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0},\quad b\mapsto\dim R_{b}.

For an irreducible closed subscheme
Z\subset B
, we define
\delta(Z)
to be
\delta(b)
with
b\in Z
a general closed point; alternatively, we have

\delta(Z)=\mathrm{min}\{\delta(b),\,b\in Z\}.

Following
[
47
]
, we say that
C\to B
is
\delta
-regular
, if we have

\delta_{Z}\leq\mathrm{codim}_{B}(Z)

for any irreducible closed
Z\subset B
.

To study fine compactified Jacobians in the relative setting, we consider the polarization stability conditions of Esteves
[
20
]
; this specializes to the stability conditions of Kass–Pagani
[
27
]
and Melo
[
39
]
for the universal curve over
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
, and the stability conditions considered in Migliorini–Shende–Viviani
[
44
]
for versal deformations of a reduced locally planar curve.

Since the explicit form of a stability condition is not important for our purpose, in the following we only describe the definitions of stability conditions and relative fine compactified Jacobians briefly; we refer to
[
40
]
for more details. We mainly need the existence of relative fine compactified Jacobians and some properties which we will emphasize.

Recall that a polarization
\phi_{b}
of degree
d
on a connected reduced curve
C_{b}
is an assignment of a rational number to each irreducible component of
C_{b}
, whose total sum over all irreducible components is
d
. A polarization as above yields a stability condition for
C_{b}
, which allows us to consider stable and semistable rank
1
torsion-free sheaves on
C_{b}
via inequalities with respect to
\phi_{b}
. A stability condition
\phi_{b}
is called
nondegenerate
if there are no strictly semistable sheaves. In this case, the
fine compactified Jacobian

\overline{J}^{\phi_{b}}_{C_{b}}
is the moduli space of
\phi_{b}
-stable rank
1
torsion-free sheaves on
C_{b}
. For two different nondegenerate stability conditions
\phi_{b},\phi^{\prime}_{b}
associated with the same curve
C_{b}
, the fine compactified Jacobians
\overline{J}^{\phi_{b}}_{C_{b}},\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}}_{C_{b}}
are birational but are not isomorphic in general; see
[
40
, Theorem B]
.

Now we consider the relative case. A polarization stability condition for the family
C\to B
is a polarization on each closed fiber
C_{b}
which is compatible with specializations
[
40
, Section 5]
. Once we fix a polarization stability condition

\phi:=\{\phi_{b},\,b\in B\}

for
C\to B
which is nondegenerate (
i.e.
, each
\phi_{b}
is nondegenerate), we can consider the
relative
fine compactified Jacobian

\pi:\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}\to B

parameterizing
(C_{b},F_{b})
with
F_{b}
a
\phi_{b}
-stable rank
1
torsion-free sheaf on
C_{b}
.

Remark 3.8
.

Strictly speaking, the relative fine compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}
may be an algebraic space. But for the purpose of proving Theorem
0.9
one can always perform an étale base change over
B
so that
\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}
stays a scheme.

For the rest of this section, we assume that we are given a family of curves
C\to B
as above, and two nondegenerate (polarization) stability conditions
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
of degree
0
, satisfying

(i)

the family
C\to B
is
\delta
-regular, and

(ii)

the total spaces of the associated relative fine compactified Jacobians

\pi:\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}\to B,\quad\pi^{\prime}:\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\to B

are nonsingular varieties.

We note that
\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi}
contains a Zariski dense open subset
J_{C}^{\phi}
parameterizing line bundles. The normalized (with respect to the section
s:B\to C
) Poincaré line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
is well-defined over

J^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}\cup\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\times_{B}J^{\phi}_{C},

and it can be extended to a maximal Cohen–Macaulay sheaf

{\mathcal{P}}:=j_{*}{\mathcal{L}}\in\mathrm{Coh}(\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi})

via the open embedding

j:J^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}\cup\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\times_{B}J^{\phi}_{C}\hookrightarrow\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi};

this was proven in
[
41
]
which was built on Arinkin’s construction
[
3
]
.

Theorem 3.9

(
[
3
,
41
]
)
.

The Fourier–Mukai transform

\mathrm{FM}_{\mathcal{P}}:D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C})\rightarrow D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C})

is an equivalence, whose inverse

\mathrm{FM}_{{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}}:D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C})\rightarrow D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C})

is induced by

{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}:={\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}\otimes p_{\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi}}^{*}\omega_{\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}/B}[g]\in D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}).

Here
p_{\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi}}:\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\to\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi}
is the projection.

For the triple
(\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C},B,\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C})
, Theorem
3.9
guarantees (a1) of Definition
3.2
with the Poincaré complex given by the Fourier–Mukai kernel
{\mathcal{P}}
. For (a2), we also need to consider the convolution kernel

(53)

{\mathcal{K}}:={\mathcal{P}}^{-1}\circ{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta_{\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}/B}^{\mathrm{sm}}}\circ({\mathcal{P}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{P}})\in D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}})

associated with
{\mathcal{P}}
, where
\Delta_{\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}/B}^{\mathrm{sm}}
is the relative small diagonal.

Next, we describe the support of
{\mathcal{K}}
. Let
B^{\circ}\subset B
be the largest open subset such that the restriction of
C\to B
, denoted by
C^{\circ}\to B^{\circ}
, is smooth. Then the restriction of both
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
to
B^{\circ}
are identical to the Jacobian fibration

\pi^{\circ}:J^{0}_{C^{\circ}}\to B^{\circ}.

The restriction of the convolution kernel
{\mathcal{K}}
over
B^{\circ}
is the structure sheaf of the relative graph
\Gamma
of the group law of
\pi^{\circ}
(denoted
G_{+}
in Section
1.3
):

\Gamma:=\{(C_{b},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3})\in J^{0}_{C^{\circ}}\times_{B}J^{0}_{C^{\circ}}\times_{B}J^{0}_{C^{\circ}},\,b\in B^{\circ},\,L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}\in\mathrm{Pic}^{0}(C_{b}),\,L_{1}\otimes L_{2}=L_{3}\}.

The following proposition is a slight extension of Arinkin’s support property
[
35
, Proposition 3.2]
. It plays the role of (the easier) Proposition
2.5
in the case of integral curves.

Proposition 3.10

(Reduced convolution class)
.

The support of the convolution kernel

\mathrm{Supp}({\mathcal{K}})\subset\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}.

is of codimension
g
. Moreover, the Zariski closure

\overline{\Gamma}\subset\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}

is the only codimension
g
irreducible component of
\mathrm{Supp}({\mathcal{K}})
; in particular, the codimension
g
support of
{\mathcal{K}}
is independent of the stability condition
\phi
.

Proof.

The proof is parallel to that of
[
35
, Proposition 3.2]
; the arguments there need to be generalized from integral curves to reduced curves, and this was already worked through in
[
41
]
(in order to establish Theorem
3.9
). For the reader’s convenience, in the following, we explain in more detail that the
\delta
-regularity assumption not only guarantees that the support of
{\mathcal{K}}
is of codimension
g
as in
[
35
, Proposition 3.2]
, but also determines the support completely as stated here in the proposition.

We consider

{\mathcal{K}}_{3}:=p_{123*}\left(p_{14}^{*}{\mathcal{P}}\otimes p_{24}^{*}{\mathcal{P}}\otimes p_{34}^{*}{\mathcal{P}}\right)\in D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}})

where the
p_{ijk},p_{ij}
are the natural projections from
\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi}
. The convolution kernel (
53
) is governed by
{\mathcal{K}}_{3}
; it suffices to show that the restriction of
{\mathcal{K}}_{3}
over
B\setminus B^{\circ}
has codimension
>g
.

We consider
b\in B\setminus B^{\circ}
(in particular,
\delta(b)>0
), and a point

(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})\in Z_{b}:=\mathrm{Supp}({\mathcal{K}}_{3})\cap(\overline{J}_{C_{b}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}})^{\times 3}.

By an identical argument as in the proof of
[
35
, Proposition 3.2]
, we have

(54)

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{3}({F_{i}}|_{C^{\mathrm{reg}}_{b}})\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{C^{\mathrm{reg}}_{b}}

where
C^{\mathrm{reg}}_{b}\subset C_{b}
is the regular locus; we note that
[
35
, Proposition 3.2]
only handles integral locally planar curves, but the proof of
[
41
, Proposition 6.3]
generalizes all the relevant arguments to reduced ones.

Next, we recall the natural action

\mu_{b}:J^{\underline{0}}_{C_{b}}\times(\overline{J}_{C_{b}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}})^{\times 3}\to(\overline{J}_{C_{b}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}})^{\times 3},\quad(L,F_{1},F_{2},F_{3})\mapsto(L\otimes F_{1},L\otimes F_{2},L\otimes F_{3}).

It is a smooth morphism of relative dimension
g
. Hence we have

(55)

\mathrm{codim}_{(\overline{J}_{C_{b}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}})^{\times 3}}\left(Z_{b}\right)=\mathrm{codim}_{J^{\underline{0}}_{C_{b}}\times(\overline{J}_{C_{b}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}})^{\times 3}}\left(\mu_{b}^{-1}(Z_{b})\right).

Let

\sigma_{b}:\mu_{b}^{-1}(Z_{b})\to(\overline{J}_{C_{b}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}})^{\times 3}

be the composition of the natural inclusion

\mu_{b}^{-1}(Z_{b})\hookrightarrow J_{C_{b}}^{\underline{0}}\times(\overline{J}_{C_{b}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}})^{\times 3}
, and the projection to the second factor; every closed fiber of
\sigma_{b}
lies in the irreducible group variety
J^{\underline{0}}_{C_{b}}
. By the proof of
[
3
, Corollary 7.6]
, (
54
) implies that any closed fiber of
\sigma_{b}
has dimension
\leq\delta(b)
, while a fiber over any closed point lying in the Zariski dense open subset

(J_{C_{b}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}})^{\times 3}\subset(\overline{J}_{C_{b}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}})^{\times 3}

is
0
-dimensional. In particular, for each
b\in B
we have

\mathrm{codim}_{J^{\underline{0}}_{C_{b}}\times(\overline{J}_{C_{b}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}})^{\times 3}}\left(\mu_{b}^{-1}(Z_{b})\right)>g-\delta(b)

which by (
55
) further implies

\mathrm{codim}_{(\overline{J}_{C_{b}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}})^{\times 3}}\left(Z_{b}\right)>g-\delta(b).

Consequently, we deduce from the
\delta
-regularity of
C\to B
that there is no irreducible codimension
g
component contained in the support of
{\mathcal{K}}_{3}
over
B\setminus B^{\circ}
. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

Arinkin’s arguments
[
3
]
can further be applied to show that
{\mathcal{K}}
is a Cohen–Macaulay sheaf supported on the Zariski closure of
\Gamma
. This is not needed for our purpose, and we leave it to the interested reader.

By Theorem
3.9
and Proposition
3.10
, the data

(\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C},B,\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C},{\mathcal{P}},{\mathcal{K}})

satisfy both (a1, a2) of Definition
3.2
. Recall the Fourier transforms
{\mathfrak{F}},{\mathfrak{F}}^{-1}
induced by
{\mathcal{P}},{\mathcal{P}}^{-1}
respectively. We note that the
\frac{1}{2}
Fourier vanishing also follows from the
\delta
-regularity of
C\to B
.

Proposition 3.12

(
\frac{1}{2}
Fourier vanishing
[
35
, Section 3.5]
)
.

For all
i+j<2g
, we have

(
\frac{1}{2}
FV1)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}^{-1}_{j}\circ{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}=0\in\mathrm{CH}_{3g-i-j+\dim B}(\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi^{\prime}}),

(
\frac{1}{2}
FV2)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}_{j}\circ{\mathfrak{F}}^{-1}_{i}=0\in\mathrm{CH}_{3g-i-j+\dim B}(\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi}\times_{B}\overline{J}_{C}^{\phi}).

Proof.

The proof is identical to that in
[
35
, Section 3.5]
for integral curves. The
\delta
-regularity is used here in a parallel way as in the proof of Proposition
3.10
. Indeed, instead of
{\mathcal{K}}_{3}
, we consider another sequence of objects

\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}(N):={\mathcal{P}}^{-1}\circ(i_{*}{\mathcal{P}})^{\otimes N}\in D^{b}\mathrm{Coh}(\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\times\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}),\quad N\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}

where
i:\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\hookrightarrow\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\times\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}
is the closed embedding. These objects are supported on
\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}
, and we can deduce the codimension estimate

\mathrm{codim}_{\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}}\left(\mathrm{Supp}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{K}}}(N))\right)\geq g

by a similar proof as for Proposition
3.10
using
\delta
-regularity. Then an argument via the Adams operations as in
[
35
, Section 3.5.2]
yields the desired (
\frac{1}{2}
FV1).

The other half (
\frac{1}{2}
FV2) can be deduced similarly; see
[
36
, Proposition 2.9]
.
∎

As before, let
{\mathcal{H}}^{\phi}_{(k)}
be the shifted perverse sheaves associated with the decomposition of the pushforward complex
\pi_{*}{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}}}
as in (
26
). The following theorem is an application of Theorem
3.6
to relative fine compactified Jacobians.

Theorem 3.13
.

With the notation as above, assume that both morphisms

\pi:\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}\to B,\quad\pi^{\prime}:\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\to B

have full support. We have the following.

(i)

The associated graded

{\mathcal{H}}^{\phi}:=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{2g}{\mathcal{H}}^{\phi}_{(k)}\in D^{b}_{c}(B)

is naturally a graded ring object induced by the cup-product on
\pi_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}}
.

(ii)

There is an isomorphism

{\mathcal{H}}^{\phi}\simeq{\mathcal{H}}^{\phi^{\prime}}\in D^{b}_{c}(B)

of graded ring objects.

Proof.

For (i), we first note that

(56)

(\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C},B,\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C},{\mathcal{P}}_{\phi\phi^{\prime}},{\mathcal{K}}_{\phi\phi^{\prime}})

is a dualizable abelian fibration; here we add subscripts for the Poincaré sheaf
{\mathcal{P}}
and the convolution kernel
{\mathcal{K}}
to indicate the dependence on the stability conditions. Indeed, as we mentioned before Proposition
3.12
, (a1, a2) of Definition
3.2
are satisfied, and (b) is also satisfied by the assumption of the theorem. Therefore, we obtain (i) from Theorem
3.5
and Proposition
3.12
.

Now we prove (ii). By setting
\phi=\phi^{\prime}
in (
56
), we obtain another dualizable abelian fibration

(57)

(\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C},B,\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C},{\mathcal{P}}_{\phi^{\prime}\phi^{\prime}},{\mathcal{K}}_{\phi^{\prime}\phi^{\prime}}).

Proposition
3.10
implies that the reduced convolution classes associated with
{\mathcal{K}}_{\phi\phi^{\prime}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{\phi^{\prime}\phi^{\prime}}
respectively coincide; both are given by

[\overline{\Gamma}]\in\mathrm{CH}_{2g+\dim B}(\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}\times_{B}\overline{J}^{\phi^{\prime}}_{C}).

Therefore, we obtain (ii) by applying Theorem
3.6
to (
56
) and (
57
).
∎

Remark 3.14
.

We note that
\delta
-regularity of
C\to B
is not sufficient to guarantee the full support property (
i.e.
, Definition
3.2
(b)) of
\pi:\overline{J}^{\phi}_{C}\to B
. For example, an elliptic fibration with reducible fibers is always
\delta
-regular, but it has
0
-dimensional supports.

3.4.
Proofs of Theorems
0.7
and
0.9

Theorems
0.7
and
0.9
are immediate consequences of Theorem
3.13
.

Proof of Theorem
0.7
.

We consider the universal curve

(58)

C_{g,n}\to\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}

over the moduli of stable marked curves (or rather, the base change to the moduli of stable marked curves with a level structure constructed in
[
1
]
). Since we assume
n\geq 1
, there is a section through the smooth locus of the family. Using this section, universal fine compactified Jacobians of different degrees are identified. So we only need to prove that

{\mathbb{H}}^{0,\phi}_{g,n}\simeq{\mathbb{H}}^{0,\phi^{\prime}}_{g,n}

with
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
nondegenerate. In view of Theorem
3.13
, it suffices to check that

(i)

the universal curve (
58
) is
\delta
-regular,

(ii)

the universal fine compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}^{0,\phi}_{g,n}
is nonsingular, and

(iii)

the morphism
\pi:\overline{J}^{0,\phi}_{g,n}\to\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
has full support,

where we assume in (ii, iii) that
\phi
is nondegenerate.

Indeed, (ii) is given by
[
27
, Corollary 4.4]
; (i) and (iii) are given by
[
44
, Fact 2.4 and Theorem 1.9]
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
0.9
.

We can use a nonsingular closed point on the reduced locally planar curve
C_{0}
to reduce to the case where both
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
are nondegenerate stability conditions of degree
0
for
C_{0}
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
3.13
, for any closed fiber
C_{b}
of the family
C\to B
there is a multiplicative perverse filtration

P_{0}H^{*}(\overline{J}^{\phi_{b}}_{C_{b}},{\mathbb{Q}})\subset P_{1}H^{*}(\overline{J}^{\phi_{b}}_{C_{b}},{\mathbb{Q}})\subset\cdots\subset P_{2g}H^{*}(\overline{J}^{\phi_{b}}_{C_{b}},{\mathbb{Q}})=H^{*}(\overline{J}^{\phi_{b}}_{C_{b}},{\mathbb{Q}}),

so that the associated graded
{\mathbb{H}}_{C_{b}}^{\phi_{b}}
is a bigraded
{\mathbb{Q}}
-algebra; for two nondegenerate stability conditions
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
, we further have an isomorphism of bigraded
{\mathbb{Q}}
-algebras:

{\mathbb{H}}^{\phi_{b}}_{C_{b}}\simeq{\mathbb{H}}^{\phi^{\prime}_{b}}_{C_{b}}.

Therefore, to prove Theorem
0.9
, it suffices to check that for any reduced locally planar curve
C_{0}
, there is a
\delta
-regular family
C\to B
containing
C_{0}
as a closed fiber, such that

(i)

any nondegenerate stability condition of degree
0
for
C_{0}
can be extended to a nondegenerate stability condition for
C\to B
,

(ii)

the relative fine compactified Jacobian associated with the nondegenerate stability condition given by (i) is nonsingular, and the morphism to the base has full support.

Indeed, both (i) and (ii) are guaranteed by taking a versal deformation
C\to B
of
C_{0}
as in
[
44
, Facts 2.3, 2.4, and Theorem 2.12]
, and the proof is complete.
∎

4.
Dependence on the stability

In this section we complete the proof of Theorem
0.6
. Discussions in Sections
4.1
-
4.3
apply generally to arbitrary
g
and
n\geq 1
. Starting from the end of Section
4.5
we specialize to the case
n=1
.

4.1.
Space of stability conditions

For the definition of stability conditions, we refer to Kass–Pagani
[
27
]
. Recall that a stability condition
\phi
is nondegenerate if there exists no strictly
\phi
-semistable multidegree.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{tl}}_{g,n}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be the locus of treelike curves; these are stable marked curves whose graph is a tree with any number of self-loops attached, and they form an open substack of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
. A stability condition
\phi
of degree
0
is called
semismall
if the trivial line bundle on the universal curve over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{tl}}_{g,n}
is
\phi
-stable. When
\phi
is semismall and nondegenerate,
\phi
-stable fine compactified Jacobians are canonically isomorphic over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{tl}}_{g,n}
.

Since we assumed
n\geq 1
, for any nondegenerate stability condition
\phi
of degree
d
we can find
\phi^{\prime}
nondegenerate and semismall such that the corresponding universal fine compactified Jacobians are isomorphic to each other:

\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n}\simeq\overline{J}^{0,\phi^{\prime}}_{g,n}.

To see this, we can use the section given by the marking to change the degree, and twist by a vertical divisor to change the bidegree for curves with
2
components and
1
separating node; this governs the locus of treelike curves
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\mathrm{tl}}_{g,n}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
by
[
27
, Corollary 3.6]
.

4.2.
Divisors on fine compactified Jacobians

From now on, we assume that
\phi
is a nondegenerate stability condition of degree
d
, and
\pi:\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n}\to\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
is the corresponding universal fine compactified Jacobian.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{g,n}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi}
be the universal curve and let
\mathcal{F}
be the universal sheaf on
\mathcal{C}_{g,n}
trivialized along the section given by the first marking. By
[
21
]
, the universal sheaf
\mathcal{F}
is the pushforward of the universal admissible line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on the universal quasistable curve

p:\mathcal{C}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qs}}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi}.

Here the admissible line bundle has degree
1
on each unstable component of
\mathcal{C}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qs}}
.
This universal family induces natural tautological classes, which generate a ring

R^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi})\subset\mathrm{CH}^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi}),

called the
tautological ring
of the universal fine compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n}
. Applying the cycle class map, we can also consider the cohomological counter-part of the tautological ring

RH^{*}(\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}})\subset H^{*}(\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}}).

Although most of the cohomological arguments in this section take place in the tautological ring
RH^{*}(\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}})
, all relevant tautological classes will be given in explicit form. For this reason, we do not provide a complete definition of the tautological ring here, and instead refer the interested reader to
[
4
,
6
]
for further details.

We first introduce several (tautological) divisor classes on
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi}
; they will be used throughout this section:

\Theta:=-\frac{1}{2}p_{*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{L})^{2}),\quad\kappa_{0,1}:=p_{*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{L})\cup c_{1}(\omega_{p,\log})),

and

\xi_{i}=x_{i}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{L}),\quad\psi_{i}=x_{i}^{*}c_{1}(\omega_{p}),\quad 1\leq i\leq n.

Here
x_{i}:\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi}\to\mathcal{C}_{g,n}^{\mathrm{qs}}
is the section corresponding to the
i
-th marking, and
\omega_{p}
(resp.
\omega_{p,\log}
) is the relative dualizing (resp. log-canonical) line bundle.

We also recall the tautological ring

RH^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}})\subset H^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}})

and the standard tautological classes

\kappa_{i},\lambda_{i}\in RH^{2i}(\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}}),\quad\psi_{i}\in RH^{2}(\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}});

see
[
50
]
. Classes on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
can also be viewed as classes on
\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n}
via the pullback

\pi^{*}:H^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}})\hookrightarrow H^{*}(\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}}).

Lemma 4.1
.

We have

RH^{2}(\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}})=H^{2}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi},{\mathbb{Q}}).

In particular, the group
H^{2}(\overline{J}_{g,1}^{d,\phi},{\mathbb{Q}})
is spanned by the classes
\Theta,\kappa_{0,1}
modulo classes pulled back from
RH^{2}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Proof.

By
[
24
]
, the Chow group
\mathrm{CH}^{1}(J_{g,n})
is spanned by tautological classes. Since
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi}
is nonsingular, the excision sequence implies that
R^{1}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi})=\mathrm{CH}^{1}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi})
. By the Lefschetz
(1,1)
theorem, it suffices to show that
H^{2,0}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi})=0
.

The rational Abel–Jacobi map
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+g}\dashrightarrow\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi}
with respect to the last
g
markings admits a resolution
a:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+g}^{\prime}\to\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi}
via a birational modification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+g}^{\prime}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+g}
with
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+g}^{\prime}
nonsingular. Since
a
is proper and surjective, the pullback map

a^{*}:H^{*}(\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}})\to H^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+g}^{\prime},{\mathbb{Q}})

is injective. As birational transformations do not affect
H^{2,0}(-)
, the vanishing of
H^{2,0}(\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n})
follows from the fact that
H^{2,0}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+g})=0
by
[
2
, Theorem 2.2]
.
∎

4.3.
Vanishing via the perverse filtration

Now we consider the perverse filtration (
1
) associated with the fibration
\pi:\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
. We recall the following proposition from
[
6
, Proposition 8.7]
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Taking cup-product with the class
\kappa_{0,1}\in H^{2}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi},{\mathbb{Q}})
satisfies

\kappa_{0,1}\cup:P_{k}H^{m}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi},{\mathbb{Q}})\to P_{k+1}H^{m+2}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi},{\mathbb{Q}}).

for any
k,m
. The same holds for the class
\xi_{i}\in H^{2}(\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}})
.

This means that the class
\kappa_{0,1}
and
\xi_{i}
have
strong perversity

1
in the sense of
[
34
]
. For example, Proposition
4.2
immediately implies

(59)

\Theta^{k}\cup\kappa^{l}_{0,1}\cup\prod_{i}\xi^{m_{i}}_{i}\in P_{2k+l+\sum_{i}m_{i}}H^{2k+2l+2\sum_{i}{m_{i}}}(\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi},{\mathbb{Q}}).

We also recall the following lemma (see
[
6
, Lemma 8.6]
) which shows that perversity implies vanishing after pushing forward.

Lemma 4.3
.

If
\alpha\in P_{2g-1}H^{*}(\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}})
, then

\pi_{*}\alpha=0\in H^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n},{\mathbb{Q}}).

As we will apply later, Proposition
4.2
and Lemma
4.3
yield vanishing of the classes

\pi_{*}(\Theta^{k}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{l})

for many values of
k,l
; this significantly simplifies our calculations.

4.4.
Outline of the proof

We prove Theorem
0.6
by contradiction. Since
\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,1}
is isomorphic to
\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi^{\prime}}
for some nondegenerate semismall stability condition
\phi^{\prime}
, we only consider stability conditions which are nondegenerate and semismall.

Let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
be nondegenerate semismall stability conditions, and suppose that there exists a graded ring isomorphism

(60)

f:H^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi_{1}},{\mathbb{Q}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi_{2}},{\mathbb{Q}})

which is linear over
H^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}})
. Since the top-degree cohomology of both fine compactified Jacobians is one-dimensional, there exists a nonzero constant
c\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
depending only on
f
such that

c\cdot\int_{\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi_{1}}}\Xi=\int_{\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi_{2}}}f(\Xi)

for all
\Xi\in H^{*}(\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi_{1}},{\mathbb{Q}})
. In particular, for any
\gamma\in H^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}})
and
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we must have

c\cdot\int_{\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi_{1}}}\Theta^{k}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{l}\cup\gamma=\int_{\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi_{2}}}f(\Theta)^{k}\cup f(\kappa_{0,1})^{l}\cup\gamma.

For
i=1,2
, let
\pi_{i}:\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi_{i}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}
be the natural projection. By the Poincaré duality for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}
, the equality of the integrals above implies

(61)

c\cdot\pi_{1*}(\Theta^{k}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{l})=\pi_{2*}(f(\Theta)^{k}\cup f(\kappa_{0,1})^{l})\in H^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}}).

For our purpose, it suffices to show that (
61
) does not hold for some choices of nondegenerate semismall stability conditions
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
.

By Lemma
4.1
, there exist unique

a,b,s,t\in{\mathbb{Q}},\quad\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in RH^{2}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}})

such that

f(\Theta)=a\Theta+b\kappa_{0,1}+\beta,\quad f(\kappa_{0,1})=s\Theta+t\kappa_{0,1}+\beta^{\prime}.

As
k
and
l
varies, the relation (
61
) imposes constraints on the coefficients
c,a,b,s,t
, and the classes
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
. In the following, we show that these constraints cannot always be satisfied as long as the genus
g
is at least
4
. Our main tool is the tautological relations obtained from the theory of universal double ramification cycles
[
4
]
over the universal Picard stack. These relations, as we will review in the next section, impose strong constraints on the classes

\pi_{i*}(\Theta^{k}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{l})\in H^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}}).

4.5.
Universal double ramification cycle relations

Let
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}
be the universal Picard stack over the moduli stack
{\mathfrak{M}}_{g,n}
of prestable curves of genus
g
with
n
markings. Let
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d}
be the connected component parameterizing line bundles of total degree
d
.

Let
\phi
be a nondegenerate stability condition of degree
d
. Using the universal admissible line bundle as in Section
4.2
, there exists a morphism to the universal Picard stack

(62)

\varphi:\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d}.

The tautological divisor classes in Section
4.2
induced by the universal admissible line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
are naturally defined on the universal Picard stack, whose pullback along
\varphi
recover the corresponding classes on the universal fine compactified Jacobians; we will use the same notation to denote these classes on
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d}
and
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi}
respectively. The universal double ramification cycle relations are on
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d}
; pulling back along (
62
) yields relations on
\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi}
for any nondegenerate stability condition
\phi
.

We first recall the universal double ramification cycle introduced in
[
4
]
(see also
[
6
]
). Let
d
be a fixed integer. Consider a tuple of integers
\mathsf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
and
b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with

\sum_{i}a_{i}=d+b(2g-2+n).

For given integers
\mathsf{a}
and
b
, the
universal double ramification cycle class

\mathsf{uniDR}_{g}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a})\in\mathrm{CH}^{c}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d})

is a certain algebraic class that governs the Abel–Jacobi theory of families of prestable curves.

We state in the following theorem some properties of the universal double ramification cycles needed in our argument. For
N\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the “multiplication by
N
” map

[N]:\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}

is given by the
N
-th tensor power of the universal line bundle.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
be fixed. Let
\mathsf{a}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
and
b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
\sum_{i}a_{i}=d+b(2g-2+n)
.

(i)

When
d=0
, the pullback

[N]^{*}\mathsf{uniDR}_{g}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a})

admits an explicit expression as a polynomial in
N
of degree
2c
.

(ii)

The class
\mathsf{uniDR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
admits an explicit expression as a polynomial in
a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
, and
b
of total degree
2c
. Here the variable
a_{1}
does not show up due to the relation between the
a_{i}
and
b
.

(iii)

For
c>g
, we have the vanishing

\mathsf{uniDR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=0\in\mathrm{CH}^{c}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d}).

Proof.

Part (i) is from
[
6
, Proposition 6.4]
. When
d=0
, (ii) is from
[
52
,
54
]
. For general
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we consider the isomorphism given by twisting by the first marking

\tau_{d}:\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0},\quad(C,\{x_{i}\},L)\to(C,\{x_{i}\},L(-dx_{1})).

Then (ii) follows from the functoriality of
\mathsf{uniDR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
under
\tau_{d}^{*}
; see
[
4
, Section 7.4]
. Part (iii) is from
[
4
, Theorem 0.8]
.
∎

We mainly focus on the case
d=0,n=1
, for which we can refine the relation in Theorem
4.4
(iii) as follows. For all
w,m\geq 0
, we have

(63)

[\mathsf{uniDR}_{g}^{c}(b;a_{1})]_{\text{weight}=w,\,\deg=m}=0,

where the weight
w
refers to the coefficient of
N^{w}
, and the degree
m
is the degree as a polynomial in
b
. The left-hand side of (
63
) admits an explicit expression in terms of tautological classes. We note that a special case of (
63
) was used in
[
5
]
.

We present the explicit forms of some special cases of (
63
), which will be used in our proof; we refer to
[
4
, Section 0.3]
for further details. We consider pairs
(\Gamma,\delta)
consisting of a prestable graph
\Gamma
of genus
g
with
n
legs, and a degree
\delta:V(\Gamma)\to{\mathbb{Z}}
on the set of vertices of
\Gamma
. Each pair defines a Picard substack
\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
with prescribed degeneration, together with a natural morphism

j_{\Gamma_{\delta}}:\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n}

of degree
|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma_{\delta})|
. We use
[\Gamma_{\delta}]
to denote the class given by the pushforward of the fundamental class
(j_{\Gamma_{\delta}})_{*}1
. For a fine compactified Jacobian
\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{g,n}
with a
\phi
-stable multidegree
\delta
on a quasistable graph
\Gamma
, let
\overline{J}^{d,\phi}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
denote the pullback of
\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
along (
62
).

Partial normalization along the edges of
\Gamma
induces a morphism

(64)

\mathfrak{Pic}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}\to\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathfrak{Pic}_{g(v),n(v),\delta(v)}\,.

We also need to consider generalizations of the classes
[\Gamma_{\delta}]
; they are natural classes of the form

[\Gamma_{\delta},\alpha]

given by the stratum
\Gamma_{\delta}
decorated by a tautological class
\alpha
pulled back from the factors
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g(v),n(v),\delta(v)}
. These generalized boundary classes appear as terms of the double ramification cycle relations.

As an example of (
63
), the relation

[\mathsf{uniDR}^{g+1}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{\mathrm{weight}=2g+1,\,\deg=1}=0

on the Picard stack
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,1,0}
is of the form

(65)

\displaystyle\frac{\Theta^{g}\cup\kappa_{0,1}}{g!}={}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle-\frac{\Theta^{g}}{g!}}\cup\sum_{g_{1}+g_{2}=g}\sum_{d+d^{\prime}=0}d(2g_{1}-1)\Big[\hbox to77.11pt{\vbox to26.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 68.97153pt\lower-19.46684pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Here the number
\delta(v)
at a vertex
v
in the graphs above refers to the assigned degree, and the number
g_{i}
below the vertex is the genus of the vertex. The infinite sum becomes finite after pulling back the relation along (
62
).

Remark 4.5
.

In the formula (
63
), we only explicitly wrote out the
main terms of the identity. Every term of the identity is of the form

\mathrm{coefficient}\cdot\Theta^{k}\cup[\Gamma_{\delta},\alpha].

As we discussed in Section
4.2
, our main application of the universal double ramification cycle relations is to handle the pushforward of monomials of
\Theta
and
\kappa_{0,1}
to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}
. After pulling back the relation via (
62
) and pushing forward to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}
, many terms vanish for dimension reasons. Namely, if the stabilization of the graph
\Gamma
has higher codimension than the pushforward class, then the corresponding term will vanish. In particular, these terms are not relevant for our arguments so we use
other terms
to denote them for notational convenience. This convention is applied throughout Section
4
for all universal double ramification cycle relations.

We recall some further relations on the stacks

\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,1,0},\quad\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,2,d},\quad\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,3,d}

given by Theorem
4.4
(iii); although we mainly consider the case
d=0,n=1
as in (
65
), we need the other two stacks to handle boundary strata.

are the

\bullet

the relation

[\mathsf{uniDR}^{g+1}_{g}(b;0,2gb+d)]_{\mathrm{deg}=1}=0

is of the form

(69)

\frac{\Theta^{g+1}}{(g+1)!}=-\frac{\Theta^{g}}{g!}\cup\left(d\xi_{2}+\frac{d^{2}}{2}\psi_{2}\right)+\text{(other terms)}.

We conclude this section by recalling a result of
[
6
]
where a closed formula for the pushforward of monomials of divisors is obtained when the perversities of the divisors sum exactly to
2g
; these classes are presented in terms of the double ramification cycle formulas over the moduli of stable curves
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}

[
26
]
. Let
\mathsf{a}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
and
b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
\sum_{i}a_{i}=b(2g-2+n)
. The trivial line bundle induces a map
e:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,0}
. The double ramification cycle formula is then defined by

\mathsf{DR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})=e^{*}\mathsf{uniDR}_{g}^{c}(b;\mathsf{a}).

By Theorem
4.4
(ii), this is a polynomial in
a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
, and
b
; we use

[\mathsf{DR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})]_{b^{m}a_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots a_{n}^{k_{n}}}

to denote the coefficient of the monomial
b^{m}a_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots a_{n}^{k_{n}}
of
\mathsf{DR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})
. We refer to
[
26
]
for explicit formulas for these classes.

Theorem 4.6

(
[
6
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
\pi:\overline{J}_{g,n}^{d,\phi}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
be the universal fine compactified Jacobian associated with a nondegenerate stability condition
\phi
where the universal line bundle is trivialized along the first marking. Let
l,m,k_{2},\cdots,k_{n}
be nonnegative integers with

2l+m+\sum_{i=2}^{n}k_{i}=2g.

Then we have

\pi_{*}\left(\frac{\Theta^{l}}{l!}\cup\frac{(-\kappa_{0,1})^{m}}{m!}\cup\prod_{i=2}^{n}\frac{\xi^{k_{i}}}{k_{i}!}\right)=(-1)^{g-l}[\mathsf{DR}^{c}_{g}(b;\mathsf{a})]_{b^{m}a_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots a_{n}^{k_{n}}};

in particular, the pushforward is independent of the stability condition
\phi
.

4.6.
Constraints

In this section, we assume the existence of the ring isomorphism (
60
) in Section
4.4
, so that the relation (
61
) holds for
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
. We derive constraints on
c,a,b,s,t\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, and
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in RH^{2}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Lemma 4.7
.

We have

a^{g}=c\neq 0,\quad s=0.

Proof.

Since
\pi_{i*}(\Theta^{g})=g!
, we have

c\cdot g!=\pi_{1*}(\Theta^{g})=\pi_{2*}((a\Theta+b\kappa_{0,1}+\beta)^{g})=a^{g}g!

where the second equality follows from (
61
) and the last equality follows from the perversity bound of Proposition
4.2
and the vanishing given by Lemma
4.3
. We conclude that
a^{g}=c
.

By a similar argument using the perversity bound of Proposition
4.2
, we have

0=c\cdot\pi_{1*}(\kappa_{0,1}^{g})=\pi_{2*}((s\Theta+t\kappa_{0,1}+\beta^{\prime})^{g})=s^{g}g!.

Therefore
s=0
.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

If
g\geq 2
, we have

a=t^{2}.

Proof.

We first prove that
[\mathsf{DR}^{1}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{b^{2}}
is a nonzero class in
H^{2}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}})
. Restricting to
{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}
, we have

[\mathsf{DR}^{1}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{b^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}(-\kappa_{1}+(2g-1)^{2}\psi_{1}).

Let
p:{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
be the forgetful map. Then we have

p_{*}(-\kappa_{1}+(2g-1)^{2}\psi_{1})=8g(g-1)^{2}\cdot[{\mathcal{M}}_{g}]\neq 0\in H^{0}({\mathcal{M}}_{g},{\mathbb{Q}}).

Therefore, we deduce from Theorem
4.6
that

(70)

\pi_{1*}\big(\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2}\big)=\pi_{2*}(\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2})\neq 0.

On the other hand, by (
61
) combined with (
59
), we obtain that

c\cdot\pi_{1*}(\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2})=\pi_{2*}((a\Theta+b\kappa_{0,1}+\beta)^{g-1}\cup(t\kappa_{0,1}+\beta^{\prime})^{2})=a^{g-1}t^{2}\cdot\pi_{2*}(\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2}),

from which we deduce

a^{g}=c=a^{g-1}t^{2}.

Here we applied Lemma
4.7
and (
70
). The desired identity is concluded.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

If
g\geq 2
, we have

\beta^{\prime}=\frac{2b}{t}[\mathsf{DR}^{1}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{b^{2}}.

Proof.

We first prove that

(71)

\pi_{1*}(\Theta^{g}\cup\kappa_{0,1})={\pi_{2*}}(\Theta^{g}\cup\kappa_{0,1})=0.

We pull back the relation (
65
) to
\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi_{i}}
and then push it forward to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}
. The left-hand side calculates (
71
). Now we consider the right-hand side: The first term does not contribute since the stability condition forces
d=d^{\prime}=0
. By dimension considerations, the only potentially contributing term from the second term is when
g_{1}=0
; however, this term also vanishes because its coefficient is a multiple of
g_{1}
. Therefore, (
71
) is concluded.

By (
61
), we obtain

0=c\cdot\pi_{1*}(\Theta^{g}\cup\kappa_{0,1})=\pi_{2*}((a\Theta+b\kappa_{0,1}+\beta)^{g}\cup(t\kappa_{0,1}+\beta^{\prime})).

Expanding the right-hand side, only the terms

(72)

\pi_{2*}(\Theta^{g}\cup\kappa_{0,1}),\quad\pi_{2*}(\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2}),\quad\pi_{2*}(\Theta^{g}\cup\beta^{\prime})

contribute by (
59
) and Lemma
4.3
. We conclude the desired formula for
\beta^{\prime}
by comparing the coefficients of the terms in (
72
) in the equation

\pi_{2*}((a\Theta+b\kappa_{0,1}+\beta)^{g}\cup(t\kappa_{0,1}+\beta^{\prime}))=0,

combined with Lemma
4.8
, (
70
), and the fact

\pi_{2*}(\Theta^{g}\cup\beta^{\prime})=g!\beta^{\prime}

given by the projection formula.
∎

Corollary 4.10
.

If
g\geq 2
, we have

t\cdot\pi_{1*}\left(\frac{\Theta^{g-1}}{(g-1)!}\cup\frac{\kappa_{0,1}^{3}}{3!}\right)=t^{2}\cdot\pi_{2*}\left(\frac{\Theta^{g-1}}{(g-1)!}\cup\frac{\kappa_{0,1}^{3}}{3!}\right)\\
+4b\cdot\left([\mathsf{DR}^{2}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{b^{4}}-\frac{1}{2}([\mathsf{DR}^{1}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{b^{2}})^{2}\right).

Proof.

By (
61
) and Lemma
4.7
, we obtain

(73)

c\cdot\pi_{1*}(\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{3})=\pi_{2*}((a\Theta+b\kappa_{0,1}+\beta)^{g-1}\cup(t\kappa_{0,1}+\beta^{\prime})^{3}).

Expanding the right-hand side, only the terms

(74)

\pi_{2*}(\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{3}),\quad\pi_{2*}(\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2}\cup\beta^{\prime}),\quad\pi_{2*}(\Theta^{g-2}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{4})

contribute by (
59
) and Lemma
4.3
.

The second term of (
74
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\pi_{2*}\big(\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2}\cup\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\pi_{2*}(\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2})\cup\beta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-2!(g-1)![\mathsf{DR}^{1}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{b^{2}}\cup\frac{2b}{t}[\mathsf{DR}^{1}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{b^{2}}

where the first equality follows from the projection formula and the second follows from (
70
) and Lemma
4.9
.
By Theorem
4.6
, the third term of (
74
) equals to

\pi_{2*}(\Theta^{g-2}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{4})=4!(g-2)![\mathsf{DR}^{2}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{b^{4}}.

We conclude the desired formula by comparing the coefficients of the terms in (
73
) and (
74
) respectively.
∎

4.7.
Explicit computation

Throughout this section we always assume that the stability condition
\phi
is nondegenerate and semismall. To complete the proof of Theorem
0.6
, we provide an explicit expression of the class

(75)

\pi_{*}(\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{3})\in H^{4}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}}),\quad\pi:\overline{J}^{0,\phi}_{g,1}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}

in terms of tautological classes on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}
. This will be achieved in Proposition
4.14
below which is the main result of this section.

The boundary stratum associated with a prestable graph containing unstable vertices corresponds to a lower-genus fine compactified Jacobian. Consider the boundary stratum
\overline{J}^{0,\phi}_{\Gamma_{\delta}}
of
\overline{J}^{0,\phi}_{g,1}
corresponding to the prestable graph
\Gamma
and the
\phi
-stable multidegree
\delta:V(\Gamma)\to{\mathbb{Z}}
. Suppose that
\Gamma
is obtained by subdividing
m
edges of a stable graph
\Gamma_{s}
; we label these
m
edges by
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}
. Let
\widetilde{\Gamma}
be the stable graph obtained from
\Gamma_{s}
by separating the edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}
. We have a canonical identification
V(\Gamma_{s})=V(\widetilde{\Gamma})
.

For each edge
e_{k}=(h_{k},h_{k}^{\prime})
, we label the two additional legs of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
by
2k
and
2k+1
(the order does not matter). By
[
6
, Lemma 2.4]
, there exists a nondegenerate stability condition
\widetilde{\phi}
of degree
-m
over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-m,1+2m}
such that the stratum
\overline{J}^{-m,\widetilde{\phi}}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\delta}}
is identified by the following diagram

(76)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}^{-m,\widetilde{\phi}}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}^{-m,\widetilde{\phi}}_{g-m,1+2m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-m,1+2m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{m}}

Here
j_{m}
denotes the morphism gluing the
2k
-th and
(2k+1)
-th markings for
1\leq k\leq m
, and
j_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}
is the gluing morphism associated with
\widetilde{\Gamma}
. In particular, over
\overline{J}^{-m,\widetilde{\phi}}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\delta}}
, there are two universal curves: the first is a family of stable curves of genus
g-m
with
1+2m
markings, and the second is a family of quasistable curves of genus
g
with one marking.

The genus
g-m
universal curve over
\overline{J}^{-m,\widetilde{\phi}}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\delta}}
together with (
64
) defines a morphism

(77)

\varphi_{s}:\overline{J}^{-m,\widetilde{\phi}}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\delta}}\to\prod_{v\in V(\widetilde{\Gamma})}\mathfrak{Pic}_{g(v),n(v),\delta(v)}.

For each vertex
v
, denote by

\Theta[v],\,\kappa_{0,1}[v],\,\xi_{i}[v],\,\psi_{i}[v]\in H^{2}(\overline{J}^{-m,\widetilde{\phi}}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\delta}},{\mathbb{Q}})

the pullback of the corresponding classes from
\mathfrak{Pic}_{g(v),n(v),\delta(v)}
along
\varphi_{s}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\jmath:\overline{J}^{-m,\widetilde{\phi}}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\delta}}\to\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi}
be the morphism in (
76
).

(i)

For
l\geq 1
, we have

\Theta^{l}\cup[\Gamma_{\delta}]=\jmath_{*}\left(\sum_{v\in V(\widetilde{\Gamma})}\Theta[v]-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{m}(\xi_{2k}+\xi_{2k+1})\right)^{l}.

(ii)

For
l\geq 1
, we have

\kappa_{0,1}^{l}\cup[\Gamma_{\delta}]=\jmath_{*}\left(\sum_{v\in V(\widetilde{\Gamma})}\kappa_{0,1}[v]\right)^{l}.

(iii)

Let
e=(h,h^{\prime})
be an edge of
\Gamma
connecting a stable vertex and an unstable vertex associated to
k
-th subdivided edge
e_{k}
, where
h^{\prime}
be the half edge attached to the unstable vertex. Then we have

[\Gamma_{\delta},\psi_{h^{\prime}}]=\jmath_{*}(\xi_{2k}-\xi_{2k+1}).

Proof.

The genus
g
universal quasistable curve over
\overline{J}^{-m,\widetilde{\phi}}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\delta}}
induces a morphism

\varphi:\overline{J}^{-m,\widetilde{\phi}}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\delta}}\to\prod_{w\in V(\Gamma)}\mathfrak{Pic}_{g(w),n(w),\delta(w)}.

For each
w\in V(\Gamma)
, we have the natural projection

p_{w}:\prod_{w\in V(\Gamma)}\mathfrak{Pic}_{g(w),n(w),\delta(w)}\to\mathfrak{Pic}_{g(w),n(w),\delta(w)}.

Consider the following relation in
\mathrm{CH}^{*}(\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d})
:

\Theta\cup[\Gamma_{\delta}]=\sum_{w\in V(\Gamma)}[\Gamma_{\delta},\varphi^{*}p_{w}^{*}\Theta].

When
w\in V(\Gamma)
is a stable vertex, then
\varphi^{*}p_{w}^{*}\Theta=\Theta[w]
.
Therefore, it is enough to consider the case when
w
is an unstable vertex of
\Gamma
. When
w
is an unstable vertex, the degree at
w
should be
1
by the stability condition. The relation
\mathsf{uniDR}^{1}_{0}(0;a+1,-a)=0
on
\mathfrak{Pic}_{0,2,1}
yields

\Theta+\frac{1}{2}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})+\text{(other terms)}=0.

After pulling back this relation along
p_{w}\circ\varphi
, the
other terms
do not contribute due to the stability conditions. Therefore, the result holds for
m=1
. The general case of (i) follows by a similar argument.

The argument for (ii) is parallel. It suffices to consider
\kappa_{0,1}[w]
with
w
an unstable vertex of
\Gamma
, and then it follows from the relation
\mathsf{uniDR}^{1}_{0}(b;a,-a)=0
on
\mathfrak{Pic}_{0,2,1}
.

Part (iii) follows from the relation
\mathsf{uniDR}^{1}_{0}(a+1,-a)=0
on
\mathfrak{Pic}_{0,2,-1}
; see
[
6
, (34)]
.
∎

We return to the computation of the pushforward (
75
); the contributions from strata away from the tree-like locus will play an important role.

We consider the classes

[\Gamma_{1}],\ldots,[\Gamma_{g-1}]\in H^{4}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}})

given by the stable graphs

\Gamma_{g_{1}}:=\Big[\hbox to80.3pt{\vbox to32.26pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 68.97153pt\lower-15.67291pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{}{}{}{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -55.99 -1.77 C -38.12 -12.09 -20.94 -12.09 -3.06 -1.77}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-65.63852pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\Big],

where
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
denote the genera of the two vertices
w_{g_{1}}
and
v_{g_{1}}
with
g_{1}+g_{2}=g-1
and
g_{1}\geq 1
.

By
[
27
, Corollary 3.6]
, the stability condition
\phi
over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}
is uniquely determined by its values at stable graphs of the form

(i)

two vertices with one edge connecting them, and

(ii)

\Gamma_{g-1}
.

For an integer
z
, let
\phi(z)
be a sequence of nondegenerate semismall stability conditions which are identical for all the graphs in (i) above, and for
\Gamma_{g-1}
we assign

\phi(z)(v_{g-1})=z+\epsilon,\quad\phi(z)(w_{g-1})=-z-\epsilon,\quad 0<\epsilon\ll\frac{1}{g-1}.

The values of
\phi(z)
on the other graphs
\Gamma_{g_{1}}
are determined by the following formula.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\phi(z)
be the nondegenerate semismall stability condition as above. Then the value of
\phi(z)
on the graph
\Gamma_{g_{1}}
is given by

\phi(z)(v_{g_{1}})=\frac{g_{1}}{(g-1)}\cdot(z+\epsilon),\quad\phi(z)(w_{g_{1}})=-\frac{g_{1}}{(g-1)}\cdot(z+\epsilon).

Proof.

Consider the stable graph
\widetilde{\Gamma}
with
g
vertices labeled
u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{g-1}
, where
g(u_{0})=0
and
g(u_{1})=\cdots=g(u_{g-1})=1
. For
i=0,\dots,g
, the vertex
u_{i}
is connected to
u_{i-1}
by one edge (with
u_{g}:=u_{0}
), and the first marking is attached to
u_{0}
.

We show that the values
\phi(z)(u_{1}),\dots,\phi(z)(u_{g-1})
are all equal. For each
1\leq i\leq g-2
, consider the contraction of graphs
\widetilde{\Gamma}\to\widetilde{\Gamma}_{i}
in which the vertices
u_{k}
for
k\neq i,i+1
are contracted to
u_{0}
. The graph
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{i}
admits a symmetry interchanging the vertices
u_{i}
and
u_{i+1}
. Since
\phi(z)
is compatible with both contractions and the symmetries of stable graphs, we obtain

\phi(z)(u_{i})=\phi(z)(u_{i+1}),\quad 1\leq i\leq g-2.

Therefore we have
\phi(z)(u_{1})=\cdots=\phi(z)(u_{g-1})
.

On the other hand, there is a contraction
\widetilde{\Gamma}\to\Gamma_{0}
contracting the vertices
u_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq g-1
to a single vertex. Compatibility of
\phi(z)
with contractions then yields

\phi(z)(u_{0})=\phi(z)(v_{g-1}).

Since
\phi(z)
is a stability condition of degree
0
, we have

\phi(z)(u_{0})+(g-1)\phi(z)(u_{1})=0,

and the desired equality follows from the compatibility of
\phi(z)
with contractions.
∎

Let

\delta:V(\Gamma)\to{\mathbb{Z}}

be a multidegree which is stable with respect to
\phi(z)
. On the graph
\Gamma_{g_{1}}
, the stability condition (see
[
27
, Definition 4.1]
) forces

\big|\delta(v_{g_{1}})-\phi(z)(v_{g_{1}})\big|\leq 1.

So there are only two possible
\phi(z)
-stable multidegrees, and we denote them by

(78)

\delta(v_{g_{1}})=\delta_{g_{1}}(z),\quad\delta(w_{g_{1}})=-\delta_{g_{1}}(z)

and

(79)

\delta(v_{g_{1}})=\delta_{g_{1}}(z)+1,\quad\delta(w_{g_{1}})=-\delta_{g_{1}}(z)-1

respectively. In particular, the constant
\delta_{g_{1}}(z)
is an invariant dependent on the stability condition
\phi(z)
.

Let
\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{\prime}
be the quasistable graph obtained by subdividing one of the edges of
\Gamma_{g_{1}}
. Denote by
v_{g_{1}}^{\prime}
and
w_{g_{1}}^{\prime}
the associated stable vertices of
\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{\prime}
. The stability condition forces

(80)

\delta(v_{g_{1}}^{\prime})=\delta_{g_{1}}(z),\quad\delta(w_{g_{1}}^{\prime})=-\delta_{g_{1}}(z)-1.

In particular, there is a unique
\phi(z)
-stable multidegree on
\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{\prime}
.

For the fibration

\pi:\overline{J}^{0,\phi(z)}_{g,1}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},

we can express the pullback of the stratum class
[\Gamma_{g_{1}}]
as

\pi^{*}[\Gamma_{g_{1}}]=[\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{+}]+[\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{-}],

where the classes
[\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{+}]
and
[\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{-}]
correspond to the strata with multidegrees (
78
) and (
79
) respectively.

Lemma 4.13
.

Assume that
1\leq g_{1}\leq g-1
.

(i)

We have the relation

\pi_{*}\left(\frac{\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}}{(g-1)!}\cup[\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{+}]\right)=2g_{1}\cdot[\Gamma_{g_{1}}].

(ii)

We have the vanishing

\pi_{*}(\Theta^{g-2}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2}\cup[\Gamma^{+}_{g_{1}}])=\pi_{*}(\Theta^{g-2}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2}\cup[\Gamma^{-}_{g_{1}}])=0.

Sketch of the proof.

The universal double ramification cycle relations provide an algorithm to calculate the classes of the form

(81)

\pi_{*}(\Theta^{k}\cup\kappa^{l}_{0,1}\cup[\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{\pm}])\in H^{4}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}}),\quad k+l=g,

which leads to the proof of both identities above. Since the full proof is lengthy but the idea is straightforward, we summarize the computational scheme here and leave the details to Section
4.9
.

A class (
81
) must be a multiple of the class
[\Gamma_{g_{1}}]
, and it suffices to determine the coefficient. First, we use Lemma
4.11
to distribute the monomial
\Theta^{k}\cup\kappa^{l}_{0,1}
among the vertices of
\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{\pm}
; then we apply the relations in Section
4.5
at each vertex. For dimension reasons, the only terms that can contribute nontrivially to the pushforward are those corresponding to the stratum
\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{\prime}
, decorated by monomials of
\Theta
divisors on stable vertices. These terms can be computed by successive applications of the universal double ramification cycle relations.
∎

Proposition 4.14
.

We have

\pi_{*}\left(\frac{\Theta^{g-1}}{(g-1)!}\cup\frac{\kappa_{0,1}^{3}}{3!}\right)=\sum_{g_{1}=1}^{g-1}a_{g_{1}}(\phi(z))\cdot[\Gamma_{g_{1}}]

where the constants
a_{g_{1}}(\phi(z))\in{\mathbb{Q}}
depend on the stability condition
\phi(z)
as

a_{g_{1}}(\phi(z))=-\frac{4(2g_{1})^{3}}{48}\left(\delta_{g_{1}}(z)-\frac{1}{2}\right).

Proof.

We multiply
\tfrac{1}{3}\kappa_{0,1}
on each side of (
66
). By (
71
) and the projection formula, the first term vanishes:

\pi_{*}(\Theta^{g}\cup\kappa_{0,1}\cup\widetilde{\kappa}_{1})=\pi_{*}(\Theta^{g}\cup\kappa_{0,1})\cup\widetilde{\kappa}_{1}=0.

The third and the fourth terms also do not contribute to the pushforward. To see this, by the vanishing Lemma
4.13
(ii), we have for any
g_{1}\geq 1
the vanishing

\pi_{*}(\Theta^{g-2}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2}\cup[\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{+}])=\pi_{*}(\Theta^{g-2}\cup\kappa_{0,1}^{2}\cup[\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{-}])=0.

When
g_{1}=0
, the corresponding term vanishes because the coefficient is a multiple of
g_{1}
. A similar argument shows that the fourth term also does not contribute to the pushforward.

Thus only the second term contributes to the pushforward. By Lemma
4.13
and (
78
), the contribution from the stratum
\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{+}
pushes forward to

\frac{-2(2g_{1})^{3}}{48}(\delta_{g_{1}}(z))^{2}\cdot[\Gamma_{g_{1}}].

Since
\pi^{*}[\Gamma_{g_{1}}]=[\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{+}]+[\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{-}]
, the projection formula implies

\pi_{*}\left(\frac{\Theta^{g-1}\cup\kappa_{0,1}}{(g-1)!}\cup[\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{-}]\right)=-2g_{1}\cdot[\Gamma_{g_{1}}].

Therefore, by (
79
), the contribution from the stratum
\Gamma_{g_{1}}^{-}
is

\frac{2(2g_{1})^{3}}{48}((\delta_{g_{1}}(z)+1)^{2})\cdot[\Gamma_{g_{1}}].

Adding the two contributions yields the formula for
a_{g_{1}}(\phi(z))
.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

The classes
[\Gamma_{1}],\ldots,[\Gamma_{g-1}]
are linearly independent in
H^{4}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Proof.

We proceed by explicit intersection theory. When
g_{1}\neq i
, the vanishing

\int_{\Gamma_{g_{1}}}\psi_{1}^{3g-3-3i}\cup\kappa_{3i-1}=0

holds for dimension reasons. On the other hand, we have

\int_{\Gamma_{i}}\psi_{1}^{3g-3-3i}\cup\kappa_{3i-1}=\int_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-1-i,3}}\psi_{1}^{3g-3-3i}\cdot\int_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{i,2}}\kappa_{3i-1}=\int_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g-1-i}}\kappa_{3g-3i-6}\cdot\int_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{i}}\kappa_{3i-3}

which is nonzero by
[
22
, (28)]
.
∎

4.8.
Proof of Theorem
0.6

Suppose that there is an isomorphism

f:H^{*}(\overline{J}^{0,\phi(z)}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H^{*}(\overline{J}^{0,\phi(z^{\prime})}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}})

of
H^{*}(\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,1},{\mathbb{Q}})
-algebras sending

f(\Theta)=a\Theta+b\kappa_{0,1}+\beta,\quad f(\kappa_{0,1})=s\Theta+t\kappa_{0,1}+\beta^{\prime}.

By Lemmas
4.7
,
4.8
, and
4.9
, we must have

s=0,\quad a=t^{2},\quad\beta^{\prime}=\frac{2b}{t}[\mathsf{DR}^{1}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{b^{2}}.

By an explicit calculation, we get

[\mathsf{DR}^{2}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{b^{4}}-\frac{1}{2}([\mathsf{DR}^{1}_{g}(b;a_{1})]_{b^{2}})^{2}=\sum_{g_{1}=1}^{g-1}\frac{(2g_{1})^{4}}{48}\cdot[\Gamma_{g_{1}}].

Therefore, by Proposition
4.14
and Lemma
4.15
, both sides of the equation in Corollary
4.10
can uniquely be expressed in terms of linear combinations of
\Gamma_{g_{1}}
with
g_{1}=1,\ldots,g-1
; comparing their coefficients, we obtain

(82)

t\left(\delta_{g_{1}}(z)-\frac{1}{2}\right)=t^{2}\left(\delta_{g_{1}}(z^{\prime})-\frac{1}{2}\right)-4b(2g_{1}),\quad g_{1}=1,\ldots,g-1.

To complete the proof, it suffices to find two degrees
z,z^{\prime}
such that (
82
) has no solutions with
b\in{\mathbb{Q}},t\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
.

In fact, we just take:

z=0,\quad z^{\prime}=2.

When
z=0
, we have
\delta_{g_{1}}(0)=0
for all
g_{1}
. On the other hand, when
z^{\prime}=2
, by (
4.7
) and Lemma
4.12
we obtain

\delta_{g_{1}}(2)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }g_{1}<\frac{g-1}{2}\\
1&\text{if }g_{1}\geq\frac{g-1}{2}.\end{cases}

For
g\geq 4
, a nontrivial rational solution
(b,t)
to (
82
) must satisfy

-\frac{t}{2}=-\frac{t^{2}}{2}-8b,\quad-\frac{t}{2}=\frac{t^{2}}{2}-8b(g-1),\quad-\frac{t}{2}=\frac{t^{2}}{2}-8b(g-2)

by considering
g_{1}=1,g-2,g-1
. This is clearly impossible which completes the proof of Theorem
0.6
.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

When
t=1
, the condition (
82
) is always satisfied for a pair of integers
z,z^{\prime}
with

z^{\prime}=z+b(2g-2),\quad b\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

This is because of the existence of an isomorphism

\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi(z)}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\overline{J}_{g,1}^{0,\phi(z^{\prime})},\quad(C,x_{1},L)\mapsto(C,x_{1},L\otimes\omega_{C,\log}^{\otimes-b}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(b(2g-1)x_{1})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha))

inducing the obvious ring isomorphism
f
. Here
\alpha
is the unique vertical divisor on the universal curve
\mathcal{C}_{g,1}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,1}
chosen so that
L\otimes\omega_{C,\log}^{\otimes-b}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(b(2g-1)x_{1})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)
has multidegree
0
over
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{tl}}_{g,1}
.
